\arg{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(G^{-})\geq\arg{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E)\geq\arg(\beta+1+\sqrt{-1}\omega)(\beta-\sqrt{-1}\omega)+\pi.

Since
G^{-}
satisfies
0<\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{-}(G_{-})\leq\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{+}(G_{-})\leq\epsilon
,
the inequality (
5.76
) contradicts Lemma
5.3
. Thus we have finished the proof of the assertion (1).

Similarly to the proof for (1), we easily see

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{D}}}(E^{\prime},E[-1])\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{D}}}(T^{\prime},F)
.
Since the
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-free part of
T^{\prime}\in{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
satisfies
\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{-}(T^{\prime})>-\epsilon
,
there exists a subobject
T^{\prime}_{+}
of
T^{\prime}
in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
such that

•

the
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-free part
T^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{fr}}}
of
T^{\prime}_{+}
satisfies
\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{-}(T^{\prime}_{{\mathrm{fr}}})>0
, and

•

the quotient
T^{\prime}_{-}:=T^{\prime}/T^{\prime}_{+}
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-free with

-\epsilon<\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{-}(T^{\prime}_{-})\leq\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{+}(T^{\prime}_{-})\leq 0
.

Then any morphism
\psi\colon T^{\prime}\to F
lifts to
\psi^{\prime}\colon T^{\prime}_{-}\to F
by
\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{+}(F)\leq 0
:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 8.21355pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-8.0191pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Since the morphism
\psi^{\prime}
is in the torsion part
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
of the abelian category
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[-1]
,
the image
\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\psi^{\prime}
of
\psi^{\prime}
in
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[-1]
is also in
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.
Truncating the Harder-Narasimhan filtration of
\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\psi^{\prime}
with respect to the
\mu_{\beta,\omega}
-semistability,
we find a subobject
H_{+}\subset\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\psi^{\prime}
such that

•

H_{+}
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-free with
-\epsilon<\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{-}\leq\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{+}(H_{+})\leq 0
,
and

•

the quotient
H_{-}:=\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\psi^{\prime}/H_{+}
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-free with
\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{+}(H_{-})\leq-\epsilon
.

Thus we have the diagram:

\textstyle{T^{\prime}_{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\psi^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{-}.}
\textstyle{F}

By the assumption for
T^{\prime}_{-}
, the morphism
T^{\prime}_{-}\to\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\psi^{\prime}
factors through
H_{+}
.
Moreover the composite
H_{+}\to\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\psi^{\prime}\to F\to E[-1]
is a mono morphism in
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
since any object in the horizontal line lives in the torsion part
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
of
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[-1]
.
Hence
H_{+}
should satisfies

(5.77)

0<\arg{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(H_{+})\leq\arg{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E[-1])\leq\arg(\beta+1+2\epsilon_{0}+\sqrt{-1}\omega)(\beta-\sqrt{-1}\omega).

Then the inequality above gives a contradiction applying Lemma
5.3
as
\epsilon_{2}=-\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon_{1}=0
.
∎

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.

Then the map
s_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\colon{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\to{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}}}
defined by

(5.78)

s_{{\mathbb{Q}}}(\beta,\omega)={{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}

is continuous.

Proof.

We first show that

{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]\subset{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(-1,2]
for any
(\beta,\omega)
and
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
in
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.
To show this,
Let
E
be in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]
and
E^{\prime}
in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]
.
We denote by
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
the heart
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
on
{\mathbf{D}}
.
Then cohomologies of
E
and of
E^{\prime}
with respect to
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
are concentrated in degree
-1
and
0
.
Hence the vanishings
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{D}}}(E[p],E^{\prime})=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{D}}}(E^{\prime},E[-p])=0
hold for any
p\geq 2
by Lemma
5.17
.
Thus we see
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]\subset{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(-1,2]
.

Take
0<\varepsilon<1/8
arbitrary.
By Proposition
5.18
, if
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
is sufficiently close, then the inclusion
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(-1+\varepsilon,2-\varepsilon]
holds.
By Corollary
5.16
, shrinking
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
if necessary, we may assume that the following holds:

(5.79)

\sup\left\{\frac{|{{Z}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E)-{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E)|}{|{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E)|}\middle|E\text{ is ${{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}$-semistable}\right\}<\sin(\pi\varepsilon).

By
[
CP10
, Proposition 4.2]
we see that
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is in the open neighborhood
B_{\varepsilon}({{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}})
and this gives the proof.
∎

6.
Non-rational coefficients stability conditions

In the previous section, we have constructed stability conditions
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
for rational points
(\beta,\omega)
in
the open set
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})
.
These stability conditions
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
could be deformed for non-rational points in
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})
by Proposition
5.15
and Theorem
2.10
.
We wish to show that the extended stability condition is given by the same construction as rational stability conditions
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
and that
the family
\{{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\mid(\beta,\omega)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})\}
is continuous for
(\beta,\omega)
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose rational stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.
Suppose
\tau=({{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}},{\mathcal{Q}})
is a stability condition obtained by a deformation of
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta_{0}},{\omega_{0}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
for a rational point
(\beta_{0},\omega_{0})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
(see also Definition
2.11
).
Any
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion object is in
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.

Proof.

Let
U
be an open neighborhood of
(\beta,\omega)
in
{\mathcal{H}}
.
By the definition of
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
for any rational point
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\in U_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
, any
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion object
T
is in
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
, in particular, in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]
.
Taking limit
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
to
(\beta,\omega)
,
we see that
T
is in
{\mathcal{Q}}[0,1]
.

Now we wish to show that
T
is in
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.
To show this, let
\phi^{-}_{\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}}(T)
be the phase of the maximal destabilizing quotient of
T
with respect to
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
for
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\in U_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
.
We claim

(6.1)

\phi^{-}_{\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}}(T)\geq\frac{1}{\pi}\arg(-\beta^{\prime}+\sqrt{-1}\omega^{\prime}).

In fact, otherwise, there exists a subobject
K
of
T
in
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
such that
the quotient
Q=T/K
is
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
-semistable with
\arg{{Z}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(Q)<\arg(-\beta^{\prime}+\sqrt{-1}\omega^{\prime})
.
By Proposition
5.13
,
Q
is in
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.
Moreover,
K
is also in
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}

since
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is the free part of
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.
Hence the sequence

(6.2)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

is exact not only in
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
but also in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
.
Thus
Q
has to be
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion.
Then the definition of
{{Z}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
implies the inequality

\arg(-\beta^{\prime}+\sqrt{-1}\omega^{\prime})\leq\arg{{Z}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(Q)

which contradicts the assumption for
Q
.

Hence
\phi^{-}(T)
has to satisfy (
6.1
).
Since one can choose arbitrary close
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\in U_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
to
(\beta,\omega)
,

T
belongs to
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose rational stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.
Suppose
\tau=({{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}},{\mathcal{Q}})
is a stability condition obtained by a deformation of
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta_{0}},{\omega_{0}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
for a rational point
(\beta_{0},\omega_{0})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
.
Then
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
and
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(-1,0]
.

Proof.

We first show
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.
Let
E
be in
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.
If
E
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion, then
E
is in
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
by Lemma
6.1
.
Now suppose that the object
E
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-free and
\mu_{\beta,\omega}
-semistable.
Since
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
is closed under extension, it is enough to show that
such an
E
belongs to
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.

Since the
\mu_{\beta,\omega}
-stability satisfies the support property by Proposition
3.6
,
there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
(\beta,\omega)
such that
\mu_{\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime}}^{-}(E)>0
for any
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\in U
.
Hence
E
is in
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
which is a subcategory of
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]
for any
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\in U_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
.
Taking the limit
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
to
(\beta,\omega)
, we see that
E
is in
{\mathcal{Q}}[0,1]
.

Now we claim the following:

Claim 6.3
.

Notations being as above, any object in
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
is quasi isomorphic to a
2
-term complex concentrated in degree
0
and
-1
with respect to the heart
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
.

Take
F\in{\mathcal{Q}}(\phi)
where
\phi\in(0,1]
.
Since the deformation of a stability condition is locally unique, we can find
a rational point
(\beta^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
such that

\arg(-\beta+\sqrt{-1}\omega)<\arg(-\beta^{\prime\prime}+\sqrt{-1}\omega^{\prime\prime})
and
the stability condition
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
satisfies
\tau\in B_{\theta}({{\Sigma}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}})
for some
\theta>0
.
Then
F
is in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(\phi-\theta,\phi+\theta)
.
Shrinking
(\beta^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})
if necessary, we may assume
\phi-\theta>0
and
\phi+\theta<\arg(-\beta+\sqrt{-1}\omega)/\pi+1
.
Then we have the sequence

[\phi-\theta,\phi+\theta]\subset(0,\arg(-\beta+\sqrt{-1}\omega)/\pi+1)\subset(0,\arg(-\beta^{\prime\prime}+\sqrt{-1}\omega^{\prime\prime})/\pi+1).

Hence the object
F
is given by an extension of objects in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]
and
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(1,\arg(-\beta^{\prime\prime}+\sqrt{-1}\omega^{\prime\prime})/\pi+1]
.
By Proposition
5.13
, any object in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(1,\arg(-\beta^{\prime\prime}+\sqrt{-1}\omega^{\prime\prime})/\pi+1]
is in
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]
, in particular, is concentrated in degree
-1
.
Since any object in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime\prime}},{\omega^{\prime\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]
is concentrated in degree
-1
and
0
, so is
F
.

To show
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
,
consider the distinguished triangle

(6.3)

\textstyle{E^{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E^{+}[1],}

where
E^{+}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
and

E^{-}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(0)
.
It is enough to show that
E^{-}
is zero.
Taking the cohomology with respect to the heart
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
,
we see that both
E^{+}
and
E^{-}
are concentrated in degree
0
part by Claim
6.3
.
Hence the triangle (
6.3
) gives a short exact sequence in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
.

Then the complex number
{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E^{-})
is in the ray
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.
If
E^{-}
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion with
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E^{-})=0
, then
E^{-}
satisfies
\tau_{2}^{R}E^{-}=0
and
\tau_{1}^{L}E^{-}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{\sigma_{1}}(1)
.
This contradicts the fact
{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E^{-})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.
Hence
E^{-}
is not
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion.
Thus we see

\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E^{-})+\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}E^{-})\neq 0
, and
the exact sequence (
6.3
) implies

0<\mu_{\beta,\omega}(E)\leq\mu_{\beta,\omega}(E^{-})

by the
\mu_{\beta,\omega}
-semistability of
E
.
Hence
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E^{-})
has to be positive and this contradicts
{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(E^{-})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
.
Thus
E^{-}
has to be zero.

We secondly show
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(-1,0]
.
Let
F
be in
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
and let
U
be an open neighborhood of
(\beta,\omega)
.
For any rational point
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})\in U_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
, we have

{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(-1,0]
and

{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(0,1]
.
Since
F
is in the heart
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
which is the extension closure of
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
and

{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
,

F
is in
{{{\mathcal{P}}}_{{\beta^{\prime}},{\omega^{\prime}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(-1,1]
.

Taking the limit
(\beta^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
to
(\beta,\omega)
, we see
F\in{\mathcal{Q}}[-1,1]
.
On the other hand any object in
{\mathcal{Q}}(-1)\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(-2,-1]
is in the extension closure of
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}[-2]
and
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}[-1]
by Claim
6.3
.
Since
F
is in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
,

F
is actually in
{\mathcal{Q}}(-1,1]
.
Thus we obtain the distinguished triangle

(6.4)

\textstyle{F^{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{0}[1],}

where
F^{0}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\in{\mathcal{Q}}(-1,0]
.
Again, by Claim
6.3
, we see that
both
F^{0}
and
F^{1}
are in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
by taking the cohomology of the sequence (
6.4
).
Hence the sequence (
6.4
) gives a short exact sequence in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
.
Since
F^{0}
is a subobject of
F
in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
,
F^{0}
is also in
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.
Hence
F^{0}
satisfies
\mu_{\beta,\omega}^{+}(F^{0})\leq 0
which implies

(6.5)

\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(F^{0})\leq 0.

On the other hand, we have

(6.6)

\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(F^{0})\geq 0

since
F\in{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.
Hence
F
must satisfy
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(F^{0})=0
and
\mathop{\mathfrak{Re}}\nolimits{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}(F^{0})\leq 0
.
This contradicts the proof of Proposition
4.2
.
Thus
F^{0}
has to be zero and hence
F
is in
{\mathcal{Q}}(-1,0]
.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose rational stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.
Suppose
\tau=({{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}},{\mathcal{Q}})
is a stability condition is a deformation of
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta_{0}},{\omega_{0}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
for a rational point
(\beta_{0},\omega_{0})\in{\mathcal{H}}_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
.
Then the heart
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
is the tilting heart
{\left\langle{{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}},{{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]}\right\rangle}
defined in Definition
3.8
.

Proof.

Let us fix the pair
(\beta,\omega)
through the proof.
Both
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
and
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}={\left\langle{{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}},{{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]}\right\rangle}
are hearts of bounded
t
-structures on
{\mathbf{D}}
, it is enough to show

(6.7)

{\left\langle{{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}},{{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]}\right\rangle}\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1].

By Proposition
6.2
,
we see both
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]
and
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
are contained in
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.
Since
{\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
is a extension closure, (
6.7
) holds.
∎

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose rational stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.

Then the map

(6.8)

s\colon{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})\to{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}}};(\beta,\omega)\mapsto{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}

is continuous, where
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is the pair defined in Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

Recall that the map is continuous on rational points in
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})
by Proposition
5.19
.
By Corollary
5.16
and
[
Bri07
, Theorem 1.2]
, there exists a stability condition
\tau
given by a deformation of
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta_{0}},{\omega_{0}}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
and whose central charge is
{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
where
(\beta_{0},\omega_{0})\in{\mathcal{(}}H^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2}))_{{\mathbb{Q}}}
.
Moreover the heart of
\tau
is just
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
by Proposition
6.4
.
Hence
\tau
is the pair
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
and the map
s
is continuous.
∎

7.
Support Property

We have obtained the continuous family

{\mathcal{S}}(\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2})=\{{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\mid(\beta,\omega)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})\}
of stability conditions on
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
.
Unfortunately it was not possible to prove the support property for
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
directly.
In this section, we show that any
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
satisfies the support property via “specialization”.
More precisely one can find a stability conditions
{{\Sigma}_{{1},{0}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
which satisfies the support property in the boundary of the family.

The following proposition is a generalization of our previous work
[
Kaw19
, Proposition 4.8]
.

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.

(1)

If
E\in{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
is
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
-semistable then
\tau_{1}^{L}(E)
is
\sigma_{1}
-semistable, and
\tau_{2}^{R}E
is
\sigma_{2}
-semistable.

(2)

The equality
\arg Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E)=\arg Z_{1}(\Phi(\tau_{2}^{R}E))
holds.

Proof.

Any
E\in{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
has the canonical decomposition:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{2}\tau_{2}^{R}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{1}\tau_{1}^{L}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Suppose that
E
is
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
-semistable.
Then, by the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
, we obtain the following:

(7.1)

\arg Z_{1}(\Phi(\tau_{2}^{R}E))=\arg Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}E)\leq\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(E)\leq Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E).

Suppose to the contrary that
\tau_{1}^{L}E
is not
\sigma_{1}
-semistable.
Then there exists a subobject
A
of
\tau_{1}^{L}E
which is
\sigma_{1}
-semistable with

(7.2)

\arg Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E)<\arg Z_{1}(A).

By Lemma
2.25
, there exists a subobject
F
of
E
such that
\tau_{1}^{L}F\cong A
and
\tau_{2}^{R}F\cong\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits g[-1]
where
g
is the composite
A\to\tau_{1}^{L}E\to\Phi(\tau_{2}^{R}E)
.
Since
E
is
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
-semistable, we have

(7.3)

\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(F)\leq\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(E).

The
\sigma_{1}
-semistability of
A
and the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
imply

\arg Z_{1}(A)\leq Z_{1}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits g)=\arg Z_{2}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits g[-1]).

By the construction of
F
, we have

(7.4)

\arg Z_{1}(A)\leq\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(F)\leq\arg Z_{2}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits g[-1]).

Then (
7.1
), (
7.2
) and (
7.4
) imply the following inequalities

(7.5)

\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(E)\leq\arg Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E)<\arg Z_{1}(A)\leq\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(F)

which contradict (
7.3
).
Hence
\tau_{1}^{L}E
is
\sigma_{1}
-semistable.

Now we wish to show that
\tau_{2}^{R}E
is
\sigma_{2}
-semistable.
Suppose to the contrary that
\tau_{2}^{R}E
is not
\sigma_{2}
-semistable.
Then there exists a quotient
B
of
\tau_{2}^{R}E
in
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
which is
\sigma_{2}
-semistable with

(7.6)

\arg Z_{2}(B)<\arg Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}E).

Then (
7.1
) implies

\arg Z_{2}(B)=\arg Z_{1}(\Phi B)<\arg Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E).

Since
\tau_{1}^{L}E
is
\sigma_{1}
-semistable, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{D}}}(i_{1}\tau_{1}^{L}E[-1],i_{2}B)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}}(\tau_{1}^{L}E,\Phi(B))=0
.
Thus the morphism
q\colon i_{2}\tau_{2}^{R}E\to i_{2}B
lifts to
E
, that is, we have the following commutative diagram in
{\mathbf{D}}
:

\textstyle{i_{2}\tau_{2}^{R}E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{i_{2}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{q}}
\textstyle{i_{2}B.}

The morphism
\bar{q}
is an epi morphism in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
since
\tau_{1}^{L}q
and
\tau_{2}^{R}q
are respectively epi morphisms in
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and in
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
.
Since
E
is
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
-semistable, we have the inequalities

(7.7)

\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(E)\leq\arg{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(i_{2}B)=\arg Z_{2}(B)

which contradicts (
7.1
) and (
7.6
).
Hence
\tau_{2}^{R}E
is
\sigma_{2}
-semistable.

For the proof of (2), suppose to contrary

\arg Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}E)\neq\arg Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E)
.
Then (
7.1
) implies

(7.8)

\arg Z_{1}(\Phi(\tau_{2}^{R}E))=\arg Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}E)<\arg Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E).

Since
\Phi(\tau_{2}^{R}E)
is
\sigma_{1}
-semistable,
Then we have

0=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(\tau_{1}^{L}E,\Phi(\tau_{2}^{R}E))\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(i_{1}\tau_{1}^{L}E,i_{2}\tau_{2}^{R}E[1])

which implies
E\cong i_{1}\tau_{1}^{L}E\oplus i_{2}\tau_{2}^{R}E
.
Since
E
is
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
-semistable, either
\tau_{1}^{L}E
or
\tau_{2}^{R}E
has to be zero.
∎

Corollary 7.2
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.

Then the supremum

(7.9)

\sup\left\{\frac{|Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}E)|}{|{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(E)|}\middle|E\text{ is ${\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}$-semistable}\right\}

is smaller than or equal to
1
.

Proof.

Suppose that
E
is
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
-semistable.
By Proposition
7.1
, we have

\displaystyle|{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(E)|

\displaystyle=|Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}E)|+|Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}E)|

which directly implies the desired assertion.
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose rational stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.

If
(\beta,\omega)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
satisfies
|\beta-1+\sqrt{-1}\omega|<\sin(\pi\epsilon)
for a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, then
the stability condition
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is in
B_{\epsilon}({\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)})

Proof.

By Corollary
7.2
, we have

||{{Z}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}-{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}||_{{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}}=||(\beta-1+\sqrt{-1}\omega)Z\circ\tau_{2}^{R}||_{{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}}<\sin(\pi\epsilon).

Let
{\mathcal{Q}}
be the slicing of the stability condition
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
.
To complete the proof, it is enough to show the tilting heart
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is contained in
{\mathcal{Q}}(-1+\epsilon,2-\epsilon]
by
[
CP10
, Proposition 4.2]
.

Recall
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is the extension closure of
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
and
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]
.
The subcategory
{{{\mathcal{T}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is clearly contained in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}={\mathcal{Q}}(0,1]
.
The inclusion

{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}\subset{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}

implies
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(1,2]
.
Hence we wish to show
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(1,2-\epsilon]
.

Now take
E\in{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.
let
F
be the maximal destabilizing quotient of
E
with respect to the stability condition
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
.
Since
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is the free part of
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
, the subobject
F
of
E
in
{\mathsf{gl}\left({{\mathcal{A}}_{1}},{{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\right)}
is also in
{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.
Lemma
5.2
implies the inequalities

0<\arg Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}F)\leq\arg(\beta+1+\sqrt{-1}\omega)
and
0<\arg Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}F)\leq\arg(\beta+\sqrt{-1}\omega)
.
Since
{\mathsf{gl}\left({Z_{1}},{Z_{2}}\right)}(F)=Z_{1}(\tau_{1}^{L}F)+Z_{2}(\tau_{2}^{R}F)
, we see

(7.10)

{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(1,1+\theta],

where
\theta=\arg(\beta+\sqrt{-1}\omega)/\pi
.
By the assumption
|\beta-1+\sqrt{-1}\omega|<\sin(\pi\epsilon)
,
if
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, then
\theta
is smaller than
1/2
.
Thus we may assume the inequality
1+\epsilon<2-\epsilon
holds for a sufficiently small
\epsilon
.
Hence we have

{{{\mathcal{F}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}[1]\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(1,1+\epsilon]\subset{\mathcal{Q}}(1,2-\epsilon].

Thus, if
\epsilon
is sufficiently small, then the tilting heart
{{{\mathcal{A}}}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is contained in

{\mathcal{Q}}(0,2-\epsilon]
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

If
(\beta,\omega)
is sufficiently close, the stability condition
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
is close to
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
.
Hence
{\mathsf{gl}\left({\sigma_{1}},{\sigma_{2}}\right)}
can be regarded as
{{\Sigma}_{{1},{0}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
.

Corollary 7.5
.

Let
{\mathbf{D}}={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}\right\rangle}
be a semiorthogonal decomposition with
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{2}})}
.
Choose rational stability conditions
\sigma_{i}=({\mathcal{A}}_{i},Z_{i})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{i}}}
such that
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
satisfies the condition
\mathop{(\mathrm{Mor{5}})}
.

If
\sigma_{i}
satisfy the support property, then any stability condition in
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})
satisfies the support property.

Proof.

Recall that the support property is open and closed by Remark
2.8
.
By Proposition
7.3
,
if
(\beta,\omega)
is sufficiently closed to
(1,0)
, then
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
satisfies the support property.
Since the set
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})
is connected, any
{{\Sigma}_{{\beta},{\omega}}^{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}}}
for
(\beta,\omega)\in{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})
satisfies the support property.
∎

8.
Construction of a path

The aim of this section is a construction of a path in the space of stability conditions on the category of morphisms.
Before the construction, let us introduce notation.
Let
({\mathbf{D}}^{\leq 0},{\mathbf{D}}^{\geq 0})
be a bounded
t
-structure of a triangulated category
{\mathbf{D}}
.
To simplify notation, we denote by
{\mathcal{A}}_{[-q,-p]}
the subcategory
{\mathbf{D}}^{\leq q}\cap{\mathbf{D}}^{\geq p}
if
p\leq q
.
The subcategory
{\mathcal{A}}_{[p,p]}
is nothing but the shift
{\mathcal{A}}[p]
of
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Moreover the subcategory
{\mathcal{A}}_{[p,p+1]}
is the extension closure of
{\mathcal{A}}[p]
and
{\mathcal{A}}[p+1]
.

Now let us specialize
{\mathbf{D}}
as the category of morphisms.
Let
{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}
be the infinity category of functors from
\Delta^{1}
to a stable infinity category
{\mathscr{C}}
.
By
[
Lur17
]
,
{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}
is stable and hence the homotopy category
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
is triangulated.
We refer to
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
as the category of morphisms in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.

Recall three functors between
{\mathscr{C}}
and
{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}
introduced in (
1.1
).
Then the adjoint pairs
d_{0}\dashv s
and
s\dashv d_{1}
determine semiorthogonal decompositions on

\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{i}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{i}}\right\rangle}
(
i\in\{0,1\}
) respectively.
Both subcategories
{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{0}
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{1}
are given by full sub category of
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
consisting of identity morphisms in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.
Moreover
{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{2}
is the full subcategory of morphisms to zero objects,
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{1}
is the full subcategory of morphisms from zero objects:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{0}={\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{1}

\displaystyle=\{[\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\colon x\to x]\mid x\in\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})\},

(8.1)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{0}

\displaystyle=\{[y\to 0]\mid y\in\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})\}=:\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}_{/0}}),\text{ and}

(8.2)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{1}

\displaystyle=\{[0\to z]\mid z\in\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})\}=:\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}_{0/}}).

These subcategories are canonically equivalent to
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.
Under the equivalence, the inclusion functor
{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{0}\to\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
(resp.
{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{1}\to\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
) is denoted by
j_{!}
(resp.
j_{*}
).

For a stability condition
\sigma=({\mathcal{A}},Z)\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{0}}}
,
set
(\sigma_{1}^{0},\sigma_{2}^{0})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{0}}}\times{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{0}}}
by
(\sigma,\sigma[-1])

and set
(\sigma_{1}^{1},\sigma_{2}^{1})\in{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{1}}}\times{\mathsf{Stab}\,{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{1}}}
by
(\sigma[1],\sigma)
.

The gluing hearts derived from the semiorthogonal decompositions
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{i}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{i}}\right\rangle}
are denoted by
d_{i}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
respectively.
Then we obtain the torsion pairs
(d_{i}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega},d_{i}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega})
on
d_{i}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
via Definition
3.8
and
the stability conditions
\Sigma_{\beta,\omega}^{\sigma_{1}^{i},\sigma_{2}^{i}}
which will be denoted by
d_{i}^{*}\sigma_{\beta,\omega}=(d_{i}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega},d_{i}^{*}Z_{\beta,\omega})
.
By the construction of the continuous map
d_{i}^{*}
,
the stability condition
d_{i}^{*}\sigma
is nothing but
\Sigma_{1,0}^{\sigma_{1}^{i},\sigma_{2}^{i}}
(see also
[
Kaw19
]
).

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a stable infinity category and let
\sigma=({\mathcal{A}},Z)
be a discrete stability condition on
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.
Take
f\in d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
and
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
arbitrary.
For any integer
p\leq-1
, the vanishing
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})}(f,g[p])=0
holds.

Proof.

Consider the semiorthogonal decomposition
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})={\left\langle{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}^{1}},{{\mathbf{D}}_{2}^{1}}\right\rangle}
associated
with
s\dashv d_{1}
.
For
[g\colon z\to w]\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
, the gluing morphism is nothing but
the canonical morphism
v\colon\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits g\to z[1]
.
Since
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
, Lemma
3.9
implies that
v
is a monomorphism in the abelian category
{\mathcal{A}}[1]
.
Hence we see that both
d_{1}g=z
and
d_{0}g=w
are in
{\mathcal{A}}
and that the morphism
g\colon z\to w
is an epi morphism in
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Moreover
f\in d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
satisfies
d_{1}f\in{\mathcal{A}}_{[-1,0]}
,
d_{0}f\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f\in{\mathcal{A}}
.
Hence we easily see

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}f,d_{1}g[p])=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{0}f,d_{0}g[p])=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}f,d_{0}g[p-1])=0
for
p\leq-2
.
Then Corollary
2.22
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,g[p])=0
for
p\leq-2
.

To discuss the case
p=-1
, suppose that
f
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion.
Then
d_{0}f
and
\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f
are both
\sigma
-torsion.
Moreover the first cohomology
H^{1}(x)
of
x=d_{1}f
is also
\sigma
-torsion since
H^{1}(x)
is a quotient of
\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f
in
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Since
d_{1}g
is
\sigma
-free, we see
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(H^{1}(x)[-1],d_{1}g[-1])=0
.
The trivial vanishing
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(H^{0}(x),d_{1}g[-1])=0
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}f,d_{1}g[-1])=0
.
Moreover the vanishings
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{0}f,d_{0}g[-1])=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}f,d_{0}g[-2])=0
hold by the cohomological degree reason.
Again Corollary
2.22
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,g[-1])=0
.

Now let us suppose
f
is not
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion.
Let
u
be the gluing morphism
u\colon d_{0}f\to\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f
of
f
.
Since
H^{0}(x)
is the kernel
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u
of
u
in
{\mathcal{A}}
, we obtain the following distinguished triangle
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u}\to f\to\bar{f}
in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
denoted by

(8.3)

\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}
\textstyle{d_{1}f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{cok}}\nolimits u[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{f}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{d_{0}f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits u.}

By the adjunction
s\dashv d_{1}
, we see

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u},g[-1])\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})}(\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u,d_{1}g[-1])=0

by degree reason.
Hence we may assume that
u\colon d_{0}f\to\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f
is a mono morphism in
{\mathcal{A}}
without loss of generality.
Then
x
is quasi-isomorphic to
(\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f/d_{0}f)[-1]
.

Take a morphism
\tau\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})}(f,g[-1])
arbitrary.
We wish to show
\tau=0
.
Since
d_{0}g[-1]
is in
{\mathcal{A}}[-1]
, it is easily see
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{0}f,d_{0}g[-1])=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}f,d_{0}g[-2])=0
.
Thus it is enough to show
d_{1}\tau=0
.

Put the morphism
d_{1}\tau[1]\colon x[1]=\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f/y\to z
in
{\mathcal{A}}
by
\tau_{1}
.
We obtain the following commutative diagram in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
:

(8.4)

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{1}[-1]}
\textstyle{z[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g[-1]}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{0}\tau}
\textstyle{w[-1].}

The commutativity implies the composite
g\circ\tau_{1}
is zero.
Hence the morphism
\tau_{1}
factors through the kernel
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits g
, which means
\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}\subset\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits g
.
Thus we obtain the following commutative diagram in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
:

(8.5)

\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{z[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g[-1]}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w[-1]}

By
[
Kaw19
, Lemma 2.11]
,
the above diagram gives a sequence
f\to j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}[-1])\to g[-1]
.
We claim that the morphism
\rho\colon f\to j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}[-1])
is an epimorphism in
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
and
the morphism
\rho^{\prime}\colon j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})\to g
is a monomorphism in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
.

Let
h
be the fiber of
\rho
in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
.
Then clearly
d_{0}h=y
, and
\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits h
is the kernel of the morphism
\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f\to\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f/y\to\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\delta
in
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Hence
h
is in
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
which implies that
\rho
is an epimorphism in
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
.

To discuss
\rho^{\prime}
, note that
j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})
is a subobject
j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits g)
in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
by
\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}\subset\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits g
.
Since
g
is epi, there exists a morphism
\iota\colon j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits g)\to g
in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
.
Then one can easily see that the morphism
\iota
is a monomorphism in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
.
Hence
j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})
is a subobject of
g
in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
.

Since
j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}[-1])
is a quotient of
f
in
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
,

j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}[-1])
is in
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
Hence
j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}[-1])
satisfies
\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits d_{0}^{*}Z_{\beta,\omega}(j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}[-1]))
is positive which implies

(8.6)

\beta\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits Z(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})-\omega\mathop{\mathfrak{Re}}\nolimits Z(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})>0.

On the other hand,
j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})
is in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
since
it is a subobject of
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
Thus the non-positivity
d_{1}^{*}Z_{\beta,\omega}(j_{!}(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}))\leq 0
implies

(8.7)

(\beta+1)\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits Z(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})-\omega\mathop{\mathfrak{Re}}\nolimits Z(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})\leq 0.

Then (
8.7
) implies
\beta\mathop{\mathfrak{Im}}\nolimits Z(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})-\omega\mathop{\mathfrak{Re}}\nolimits Z(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})\leq 0
since
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits Z(\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1})
is non-negative.
This clearly contradicts (
8.6
).
Hence
\mathop{\mathsf{im}}\nolimits\tau_{1}
is zero which implies
d_{1}\tau=0
.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a stable infinity category and let
\sigma=({\mathcal{A}},Z)
be a discrete stability condition on
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.
Take
f\in d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
and
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
arbitrary.
For any integer
p\leq 0
, the vanishing
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})}(g,f[p])=0
holds.

Proof.

Since
f
is in
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
, the gluing morphism
d_{0}f\to\mathop{\mathsf{cof}}\nolimits f
is mono by Lemma
2.21
.
Hence
d_{1}f
is in
{\mathcal{A}}[-1]
.
Since
g
is in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
, in particular in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
,

d_{1}g
is in
{\mathcal{A}}
and
d_{0}g
is in
{\mathcal{A}}_{[0,1]}
.
Hence Corollary
2.22
implies the vanishing
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(g,f[p])=0
for
p\leq-1
.

To discuss the case
p=0
, suppose that
g
is
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion.
Then
d_{1}g
and
\mathop{\mathsf{fib}}\nolimits g
are
\sigma
-torsion objects in
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Moreover the
0
-th cohomology
H^{0}(d_{0}g)
of
d_{0}g
is
\sigma
-torsion since it is a quotient of the
\sigma
-torsion object
d_{1}g
.
Then we see
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{0}g,d_{0}f)=0
by the
\sigma
-freeness of
d_{0}f
.
In addition the vanishings
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}g,d_{1}f)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}g,d_{0}f[-1])=0
hold by the cohomological degree reason.
Thus Corollary
2.22
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(g,f)=0
.

Now suppose that
g
is not
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
-torsion.
Let
u
be the universal morphism
\mathop{\mathsf{fib}}\nolimits g\to d_{1}g
.
Then there exists a diagram of the distinguished triangle in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
:

(8.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{g}}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{w\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathop{\mathsf{cok}}\nolimits u.}

Put the right horizontal arrow by
[\bar{g}\colon z\to\mathop{\mathsf{cok}}\nolimits u]
which gives an object in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
.
By
[
Kaw19
, Lemma 2.11]
, the above digram gives a distinguished triangle in
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
, in particular, a short exact sequence in
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
.
Since the vanishings
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(j_{*}(\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u[1]),f)\cong\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits u[1],d_{0}f)=0
hold by the fact
d_{0}f\in{\mathcal{A}}
,
it is enough to show
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(\bar{g},f)=0
.

Take
\bar{\varphi}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(\bar{g},f)
.
Since
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}\bar{g},d_{1}f)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}\bar{g},d_{0}f[-1])=0
hold by the cohomological degree reason,
it is enough to show
d_{0}\bar{\varphi}=0
.
Since
(d_{0}\bar{\varphi})\circ\bar{g}=f\circ(d_{1}\bar{\varphi})=0
,

\bar{g}
factors through the kernel
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits d_{0}\bar{\varphi}
.
Since
\bar{g}
is an epimorphism in
{\mathcal{A}}
, the canonical morphism
\mathop{\mathsf{ker}}\nolimits d_{0}\bar{\varphi}\to\mathop{\mathsf{cok}}\nolimits u
has to be an epimorphism.
Thus
d_{0}\bar{\varphi}
is zero morphism.
∎

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a stable infinity category and let
\sigma=({\mathcal{A}},Z)
be a locally finite stability condition on
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.

(1)

d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}\subset(d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega})_{[-1,0]}
.

(2)

d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}\subset(d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega})_{[-2,-1]}
.

Proof.

Any object
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega}
has a decomposition

\textstyle{H^{-1}(g)[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{g\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(g)}

with respect to the heart
d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}
where

H^{-1}(g)\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
and
H^{0}(g)\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
Clearly
d_{1}(H^{0}(g))
is in
{\mathcal{A}}
, and
d_{0}(H^{0}(g))
is in
{\mathcal{A}}_{[0,1]}
.
By Lemma
3.9
, both
d_{0}(H^{-1}(g))
and
d_{1}(H^{-1}(g))
are in
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Hence both
d_{0}g
and
d_{1}g
are in
{\mathcal{A}}_{[0,1]}
.

Take
f\in d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
and
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
To prove the assertion (1), it is enough to show

(1a)

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(g[p],f)=0
for
p\geq 1
and

(1b)

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,g[q])=0
for
q\leq-2
.

Since
d_{1}g[p]
and
d_{0}g[p]
are in
{\mathcal{A}}_{[p,p+1]}
,

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}g[p],d_{1}f)
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{0}g[p],d_{0}f)
, and
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}g[p],d_{0}f[-1])
vanish.
Then Corollary
2.22
implies (1a).

Now suppose
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
Then
d_{1}g[q]\in{\mathcal{A}}[q]
and
d_{0}g\in{\mathcal{A}}_{[q,q+1]}
.
The assumption
q\leq-2
implies the vanishings

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}f,d_{1}g[q])=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{0}f,d_{0}g[q])=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(d_{1}f,d_{0}g[q-1])=0.

Corollary
2.22
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,g[q])=0
for
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
Now Lemma
8.1
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,g[q])=0
for
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}[1]
.
Thus we have proven (1b).

Take
f\in d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}
and
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
To prove the assertion (2), it is enough to show

(2a)

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(g[p],f)=0
for
p\geq 0
and

(2b)

\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(f,g[q])=0
for
q\leq-3
.

Lemma
8.2
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(g[p],f)=0
for
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
.
Suppose
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}[1]
.
Then
d_{1}g[p]
and
d_{0}g[p]
are in
{\mathcal{A}}[p+1]
.
Since
f
is in
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
,
d_{1}f
is in
{\mathcal{A}}[-1]
and
d_{0}f
is in
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Corollary
2.22
implies
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(g[p],f)=0
for
g\in d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}[1]
.
This gives the proof of claim (2a).

To prove (2b), recall
g[q]
satisfies
that both
d_{0}^{*}g[q]
and
d_{1}g[q]
are in
{\mathcal{A}}_{[q,q+1]}
.
Since
f
satisfies
d_{0}f\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
d_{1}f\in{\mathcal{A}}[-1]
,
Corollary
2.22
implies the claim (2b).
∎

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a stable infinity category and let
\sigma=({\mathcal{A}},Z)
be a locally finite stability condition on
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.
Then
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega}\subset(d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega})_{[-1,0]}
.

Proof.

Since the heart
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{\beta,\omega}
is the extension closure of
d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta,\omega}
and

d_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}_{\beta,\omega}[1]
, the assertion follows from Proposition
8.3
.
∎

Theorem 8.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a stable infinity category and let
\sigma=({\mathcal{A}},Z)
be a locally finite stability condition on
\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})
.
If
\sigma
is rational,
then
d_{0}^{*}\sigma
and
d_{1}^{*}\sigma
are path connected in
{\mathsf{Stab}\,{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})}}
.

Proof.

Let
p
be a path in
\bar{{\mathcal{H}}}
given by

p\colon[0,1]\to\bar{{\mathcal{H}}};p(t)=\left(\cos(2\pi t/3),\sin(2\pi t/3)\right).

Set
\epsilon_{1}
as
1/3
and
\epsilon_{2}
as
-1/2
in
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(\epsilon_{1})\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(\epsilon_{2})
.
Then the domain
{\mathcal{H}}^{+}(1/3)\cap{\mathcal{H}}^{-}(-1/2)
contains the image
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits p
of the path
p
.
Hence the set

\{d_{0}^{*}\sigma_{\beta,\omega}\mid(\beta,\omega)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits p\}

of stability conditions
gives a continuous family.

Now, when
(\beta,\omega)\in\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\nolimits p
, we denote by
d_{i}^{*}\sigma_{p(t)}=(d_{i}^{*}Z_{p(t)},d_{i}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{p(t)})
instead of
d_{i}^{*}\sigma_{\beta,\omega}
.
By direct calculation, one can check that

d_{0}^{*}Z_{p(2\pi/3)}(f)=\exp(2\pi\sqrt{-1}/3)d_{1}^{*}Z_{p(2\pi/3)}(f)

for any
f\in\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})
.
Corollary
8.4
implies that the heart of the stability condition
d_{0}^{*}\sigma_{p(2\pi/3)}
is contained in

(d_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}_{p(2\pi/3)})_{[-1,1]}
.
Then
[
CP10
, Proposition 4.1]
implies the desired assertion.
∎

Remark 8.6
.

If
\sigma
is full then one can assume that
\sigma
is rational.
Hence for any full stability
\sigma
,
d_{0}^{*}\sigma
is path connected to
d_{1}^{*}\sigma
.
Theorem
8.5
is a generalization of our previous result
[
Kaw19
, Theorem 1.2]
.
Hence we obtain the following:

Corollary 8.7
.

Let
{\mathscr{C}}
be a stable infinity category and let
{\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{fl}}\,{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})}}
be the space of full stability conditions.
Then the images of maps

d_{0}^{*},d_{1}^{*}\colon{\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{fl}}\,{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})}}\rightrightarrows{\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{fl}}\,{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})}}

are path connected to each other.
In other word,
the restricted map
d_{0}^{*}
and
d_{1}^{*}
gives the same map from the
0
-th homotopy of
{\mathsf{Stab}\,{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})}}
to that of
{\mathsf{Stab}\,{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}^{\Delta^{1}}})}}

Proof.

Recall that the
0
-th homotopy
\pi_{0}(X)
of a topological space
X
is nothing but the set of connected components of
X
.
Let
[x]
be the equivalence class of a point
x\in X
with respect to path connectedness.
By Theorem
8.5
,
[d_{0}^{*}\sigma]
is the same as
[d_{1}^{*}\sigma]
for
\sigma\in{\mathsf{Stab}^{\mathsf{fl}}\,{\mathrm{h}({{\mathscr{C}}})}}
.
∎
